It is a common proverb: That only masters are permitted to pass judgment <term>judgment (to judge)</term> <note>Original: *judiciren*, a Germanization of the Latin *iudicare*, meaning to pass professional or scholarly judgment.</note> upon the arts <note>In this 17th-century context, "arts" (*Künsten*) refers to specialized branches of knowledge or sciences, such as alchemy, medicine, or philosophy.</note>, because they understand them correctly and, for that reason, are able to judge them rightly. For just as a blind man cannot show the way to another blind man, and as the blind can neither speak of colors nor distinguish between them: so it is also impossible for anyone to speak or write of those arts of which he has no

<meta>catchword: foundation (grund)</meta>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument from the previous leaf regarding the necessity of expertise. ->To the Favorable Reader.<-

foundation nor understanding, nor has he throughout his life concerned himself with them, much less learned them and practiced therein. *For there is no desire for what is unknown* <note>original: "Ignoti enim nulla cupido." This Latin maxim was a common rhetorical device used to explain that human will is only moved by what the intellect first perceives.</note>. What one does not know, one does not desire.

It is also so provided in Nature, that if a thing is not naturally disposed or ordered toward a purpose, one will never be able to bring it to that state in all eternity. From radish seeds <term>radish seeds (Ruberettigsamen)</term> <note>The author refers to the *Ruberettig*, a large winter radish or horseradish. In early modern botanical metaphors, the "seed" represents the inherent potential or "essence" of a thing.</note>—even if you pour or bury a great many of them together into a small pit in the field—no house or donkey will grow for you. ## Preface

# To the Favorable
## Reader.

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'E' featuring a decorative woodcut design with foliage and figures.</image-desc>
There is a common proverb: that it is permitted to masters <term>masters (Meistern)</term> <note>In the early modern period, a "Master" was someone who had attained the highest level of proficiency in a guild or craft, legally entitled to teach others and judge the quality of work.</note> alone to judge <term>judge (judiciren)</term> <note>Derived from the Latin *iudicare*. The author uses this term to denote a formal, authoritative evaluation rather than a casual opinion.</note> the arts <term>the arts (Künsten)</term> <note>This refers not just to "fine arts" like painting, but to all technical skills, sciences, and crafts—the "know-how" of a specific field.</note>, because they understand them rightly and are therefore able to pass correct judgment upon them. For just as a blind man cannot show another blind man the way, and just as those who are blind cannot speak of colors nor distinguish them: so it is also impossible that one could speak or write of the arts of which he has no foundation <note>The original German text breaks off here with the word "grund" (foundation), which matches the catchword for the following page.</note>

<meta>catchword: foundation (grund)</meta>

<summary>In this opening to the preface, the author argues that only those with mastery and a solid foundation in a subject are qualified to judge or write about it, using the metaphor of a blind man unable to perceive color.</summary>
<keywords>preface, expertise, judgment, masters, arts and sciences, foundation of knowledge, epistemology</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from the previous page, completing the thought that those without training lack both the foundation and the desire to judge the arts correctly.</meta>

foundation nor understanding; nor has he concerned himself with such things throughout his life, much less learned them or practiced therein. *For there is no desire for what is unknown.* <note>original: "Ignoti enim nulla cupido." This Latin proverb, famously used by the poet Ovid, suggests that human desire is limited by knowledge; we cannot crave what we cannot conceive.</note> What one does not know, one does not desire.

It is also thus provided in Nature <term>Nature (Natur)</term> <note>In this context, "Nature" refers to the inherent, God-given properties and limitations of physical things.</note>: if a thing is not naturally inclined or destined for a certain purpose, one will never be able to bring it to that end in all eternity. From radish seed <term>radish seed (Ruberettig-samen)</term> <note>Specifically referring to a large black radish or horseradish, a common crop in German gardens of the time.</note>—even if you pour a great many of them together into a small pit <term>small pit (Grüblein)</term> in the field or bury them—no house or donkey will grow for you. Instead, Nature produces from the seed a large radish, which grows forth in its own time. What a person is not capable of or does not understand, he should not presume to undertake <note>The text breaks off here at the catchword "vntre," likely continuing the thought that one should not meddle in matters beyond their skill or natural capacity.</note>

<meta>catchword: undertake (vntre)</meta>

<summary>The author uses biological metaphors, specifically the growth of a radish, to argue that expertise and inherent nature cannot be forced or faked. He concludes that one should not attempt to judge or practice arts for which they have no natural "seed" or training.</summary>

# Preface /

...undertake. For it cannot fail but that he must bring forth **absurd things** <note>The original German "vngereimet ding" literally means "unrhymed things," a common idiom for nonsense or inconsistencies.</note> and go astray; so that when a question about **windmills** is posed, he will answer regarding **watermills**. Yet one nevertheless sometimes finds **clever-clogs** <term>clever-clogs (Klüglinge)</term> and **conceited deep-thinkers** <term>deep-thinkers (Tieffdünckel)</term> who imagine they know everything; but they often fall short by a great **Oidrian farmer's stride** <term>Oidrian farmer's stride (Euterischen Bawernschrits)</term> <note>A regional idiom referring to the inhabitants of the Euter Valley in the Odenwald, who were historically portrayed in German folk humor as clumsy or rustic.</note>. They stumble in like a peasant in great boots, who does not watch his step carefully and lands in the puddles rather than on dry ground.

It is written of the famous and skillful painter *Apelles* <note>Apelles of Kos (4th century BCE) was the most renowned painter of Ancient Greece, often cited as a model of artistic perfection.</note> that he was accustomed to placing his paintings publicly in the streets, so that he might [hear] all sorts of **judgments** <term>judgments (Iudicia)</term> <note>original: "Iudicia" (Latin). This refers to formal opinions or critical assessments.</note> and talk regarding them from those who passed by...

### To the Favorable Reader.

...take note; especially what concerns the **attire** <term>attire (Habit)</term> <note>original: "Habit" (from Latin *habitus*), referring to the style of dress or costume of the time.</note> and the clothing, so that he might learn something from the masters from their **judgments** <term>judgments (judiciis)</term> <note>original: "judiciis" (Latin). In this context, it refers to the critical assessments or expert opinions of others.</note>.

When a coarse **shoemaker** <term>shoemaker (Schuster)</term> once passed by and looked at the **paintings** <term>paintings (Gemelde)</term>, he inspected the **boots** <term>boots (Stieffeln)</term> which Apelles had applied to a figure with colors; and as he found nothing therein to change, he then began to criticize the clothing. When Apelles, who had kept himself hidden behind the paintings, heard this, he immediately spoke to him: **"Shoemaker, not above your boots."** <note>This is a translation of the Latin proverb *Sutor, ne ultra crepidam* ("Shoemaker, not beyond the shoe"). It is a classic warning against offering criticism outside one's area of expertise.</note> With this, he indicated to him that he should not judge that which he had not learned nor understood.

This shoemaker has left many sons and daughters behind him, who even to this day still follow their father and presume—

# Preface.

to judge things and arts for which they have not even **washed their hands** <note>This is a metaphorical expression meaning to approach a subject without the necessary preparation, purity, or basic respect required for the craft.</note>, much less actually laid a hand upon the work itself. **God** the Almighty has many and various **gifts** <term>gifts (Gaben)</term>, which he distributes and shares with mankind out of his **Divine Treasury** and **cabinet of curiosities** <term>cabinet of curiosities (Kunstkammer)</term> <note>original: "Kunstkammer." These were early predecessors to museums, containing a mix of art, scientific instruments, and natural wonders.</note>.

However, He does not give everything to one person; otherwise, that individual would become too **haughty** <term>haughty (hoffertig)</term> and despise others alongside him. For this reason, no one can nor should prescribe to God what gifts He should share and bestow upon each person.

We Christians believe that all **wisdom of nature** <term>wisdom of nature (Weißheit der Natur)</term> is contained within the pagan Aristotle; and once we have studied his "blind" **Physics** <term>Physics (Physicam)</term> <note>Aristotle's *Physics* was the standard textbook for natural science in the Middle Ages and Renaissance, though later thinkers often criticized it as being "blind" to spiritual or newer empirical truths.</note>, we believe ourselves to be **bold masters** <note>original: "Meister Kecks." # To the Favorable Reader!

[not] hear the grass grow. But when the **Aristotelian Master** <note>original: "Dominus Aristotelicus." A title given to a scholar who strictly adheres to Aristotle’s teachings.</note> is meant to solve **Samson’s riddle** <note>original: "Frage Samsonis." Referring to the biblical story in Judges 14:14, where bees and honey were found in the carcass of a lion.</note> through his master's art—how bees grow out of a lion—there is **no one at home** <note>A German idiom ("da ist niemand daheim") meaning the person has no understanding of the matter or is intellectually empty.</note>, and he answers meanwhile **with silence** <note>original: "ex Tacito." This is a play on words; while referencing the Roman historian Tacitus, the Latin "tacitus" also means silent. The author is mocking the scholar for having no answer.</note>.

In ancient times, the **ancestors** <term>ancestors (Altväter)</term> spoke and held conversations about nature during their gatherings and **banquets** <term>banquets (Gastereyen)</term>. Today, however, in the **universities** <term>universities (hohen Schulen)</term>, they do not even know what nature is; instead, each one merely parrots his master **Aristotle** <note>original: "Aristoteli."</note>, and when the **disputation** <term>disputation (Disputation)</term> <note>A formal academic debate used in universities to defend a thesis.</note> is over, he is much more inexperienced and unskilled than when he first arrived.

Since we gain so little practical skill from **Aristotle** <note>original: "Aristotele."</note>, why then do we cling so diligently to his teachings, as if we had been baptized in his name alone? Is...

<summary>The author criticizes university scholars for their blind devotion to Aristotle, arguing that they lack a true understanding of nature and fail to solve practical or philosophical mysteries.</summary>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of scholastic education. The catchword from the previous page, "nicht" (not), completes the opening thought: that it is shameful for men to judge things they have not studied or practiced.</meta>

# Preface:

[not] to judge matters and arts for which they have not even washed their hands <note>An idiom suggesting a lack of preparation or ritual purification before approaching a sacred or complex subject.</note>, much less laid a hand upon them in practice. **God** the Almighty has many and various gifts which He distributes and imparts to mankind from His divine treasure and **art-chamber** <term>art-chamber (Kunstkammer)</term> <note>A "Kunstkammer" was a cabinet of curiosities—a collection of rare, scientific, and artistic objects intended to represent the world in miniature.</note>. He does not, however, give everything to one person, otherwise that person would become too haughty and despise others alongside him. # To the Favorable Reader.

Roger Bacon was an excellent philosopher <note>original: "Philosophus"</note>, and he presents his writings from the foundation of Nature, as is fitting and proper for a philosopher. To reveal everything clearly is not good, because of the world's wickedness, which misuses even the best and most precious things, so that they become as harmful as a wicked poison.

The foundation of transmutation <note>The alchemical process of changing base metals into noble ones like gold or silver</note> is contained within this little treatise, and everything is derived from its source; likewise, everything is placed clearly enough before one's eyes, by which natural works proceed and are accomplished; whoever possesses reason and wisdom <note>original: "vernunfft vñ wiß"</note> may make use of them.

Finally, I must also remind the favorable reader that some wish to attribute these treatises on the *Tincture of the Sun* <note>original: "Tinctura Solis"—the "Sun" is the alchemical symbol for gold</note>, *Vitriol*, and *Antimony* to Isaac Hollandus <note>A famous, though somewhat mysterious, Dutch alchemist whose work was often contemporary with or confused with Bacon's</note>; which I on...

<summary>The author introduces Roger Bacon as a master of natural philosophy and justifies the use of obscured language in alchemy to prevent the "wicked" from misusing powerful knowledge. He also notes a scholarly dispute regarding whether these specific texts were written by Bacon or Isaac Hollandus.</summary>

### Preface

leave it in its place; but because for a long
time they have been found written under the

# Of the Highly Learned Lord

# Preface.

# By Roger Bacon.

Favorable, dear reader: the ancient philosophers and naturalists <note>The original German "Naturkündiger" refers to those who study the secrets and workings of the natural world.</note> have written their books in many various ways and manners whenever they intended to conceal an excellent and high art <note>The "high art" refers to the Great Work of alchemy: the purification of metals and the search for the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, so that it might not become common knowledge. For this reason, they have [conveyed] the art through parables and allegories, and indeed in the manner of riddles <note>original: "Räzelsweise"</note>...

<sig>B</sig>

# Preface

...described in such ways, and just as if
hidden in a dark fog and
left to posterity, as
if they almost did not wish to grant them the Art <note>In this context, the "Art" refers to the Great Work of alchemy: the purification of matter and the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> at all.
Yet all of this has happened
for important reasons: Therefore, I wish herewith
to kindly remind the lover of the Art,
that he should diligently read this my little book—
which I have divided into seven chapters and
which deals with the transformation of metals <term>Metallen verendrung: the alchemical process of transmutation, particularly turning base metals into noble ones like gold or silver</term>—
above all other writings, and let it be commended to him,
and from it draw a thorough foundation for his understanding,
so that he may well weigh and diligently consider
the beginning, the middle, and the end,
so that he may fathom the high and hidden things
contained herein, and satisfy his spirit <note>original: "Gemüthe"</note> in all things.

<meta>This page follows the preface where Roger Bacon introduced his seven-chapter treatise. # What Alchemy Is
## The First Chapter.

---

<image-desc>Large decorative initial letter E containing a figure amidst floral scrollwork.</image-desc>Alchemy is described in various ways by the ancient *philosophers* and writers, which we wish to briefly recount and consider in this chapter. *Hermes*, the ancient *philosopher* and natural scientist, describes this art of alchemy with the following words:

> Alchemy is an art of bodies <note>In alchemical texts, "bodies" (Cörper) usually refers to solid substances, specifically metals.</note> which proceeds simply out of and through one thing or root, in which it effectively assembles what is pure and precious through preceding *speculation* <note>original: "speculation"; here meaning theoretical contemplation or mental study of nature's laws</note> or observation of natural effects, imitates nature, and transforms such things into a better essence through a mixture permitted by nature.

Another *philosopher*, however, describes alchemy with these words:

> Alchemy is an art that teaches how one can transform all metals into one another. This, however, occurs through their own *medicine* <term>Medicin: in alchemy, the "medicine" or "tincture" refers to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone, which was believed to "heal" base metals by removing their impurities to turn them into gold.</term>, as

<summary>This page defines alchemy through the perspectives of ancient authorities, framing it as a natural art that imitates nature to purify substances and transform metals using a specific "medicine."</summary>
<keywords>definition of alchemy, Hermes Trismegistus, transformation of metals, alchemical medicine, philosophical speculation, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues from the first chapter's exploration of definitions, concluding the author's own summary of alchemy before transitioning into the second chapter's focus on the natural origins of minerals and the "seed" of metals.</meta>

->MIRROR OF ALCHEMY<-

...this is described sufficiently in many of the *philosophers'* books. Therefore, I state that alchemy is an art that teaches how one should prepare and ready a *medicine*, which is called the *Elixir*, through which all imperfect metals—when it is cast upon them—are made perfect and *perfected* <note>original: "perficirt"; meaning to bring to its highest, most refined state, like turning lead into gold.</note> almost in an instant.

# Concerning the Natural Beginning Things, and How the *Minerals* Are Born.

## The Second Chapter.

Secondly, I must provide a detailed explanation of what the natural beginning things are, and how the *minerals* are born and perfectly attain their end. From these two beginning things <note>referring to quicksilver and sulfur from the previous page</note> all metals and minerals take their origin and are born, of which many types and kinds are found. But I say this: that Nature always sets for herself the goal, and works toward it, of how she might bring and work everything to the **perfection of Gold**. That this, however, does not happen, and she works other metals instead, is caused by the *accidents* <note>In alchemical philosophy, "accidents" are external factors like impure soil, improper heat, or sulfurous vapors that interfere with the natural "growth" of gold in the earth.</note> which hinder it during birth, so that another metal breaks forth instead of Gold, as all the *philosophers* <note>The "philosophers" refers to ancient and medieval authorities on alchemy and natural science.</note> clearly demonstrate and describe in their books. 

For according to whether the beginning things—namely **quicksilver** and **sulfur**—are pure or impure, so are pure and impure metals born from them: namely **Gold**, **Silber**, **Tin**, **Lead**, **Copper**, and **Iron**. How the nature of each of these behaves and is constituted—namely, how pure or impure each is, or what superfluous impurity or purity it attained in its birth—of that I give this following report.

# How the Operation or Manual Labor and the Fire Should Be Governed and Continued.

## The Fourth Chapter.

<image-desc>A row of decorative fleurons precedes the chapter text. A large ornamental woodcut drop cap 'I' is used to begin the first word, decorated with scrolling vines and foliage.</image-desc>

I hold it to be so, dear reader—provided you possess wit or understanding, and your spirit and sense are not clouded with blindness—that you will have well understood from my speech and writing what the true <term>matter</term> <note>original: *Materia*; the raw physical substance used at the start of the alchemical process</note> of the <term>philosophers</term> <note>original: *Philosophen*; the historical masters of alchemical wisdom</note> is, from which the <term>blessed Stone of the Wise</term> <note>the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals and prolonging life</note> is made, and upon which the entire art of <term>alchemy</term> <note>original: *Alchimey*</note> stands and is founded. This is when we wish to bring that which is imperfect to <term>perfection</term> <note>original: *Vollkommenheit*; in alchemy, this usually refers to the "ripening" of base metals into gold</note>, which occurs only through something that is raised and exalted above the perfect

I have now fulfilled my promise; namely, how one should prepare and complete the excellent heavenly red and white <term>Elixir</term> <note>the "Medicine" or Philosopher's Stone in its finished form</note> through this high mastery. Finally, I must also treat and describe to you the <term>Projection</term> <note>the final act of casting a tiny amount of the Elixir onto molten base metal to transform it</note>, which is the final goal and the fruit of this work, and which gladdens the practitioner with great and longed-for joy. 

The red <term>Elixir</term> colors an infinite amount red, and transforms all <term>metals</term> into the best and purest gold. The white <term>Elixir</term>, however, also colors so much that it is uncountable, and gives all metals the perfect whiteness—that is, it changes them into the best silver. 

One must also know here that one metal is further removed from <term>perfection</term> <note>original: *perfection*; in alchemical theory, all metals "strive" to become gold; gold is considered "perfect" while others are "imperfect" or "sick"</note> than another. Likewise, one is more closely related to <term>perfection</term> than the other. And although every <term>metal</term> can be brought to <term>perfection</term> and completeness through the <term>Elixir</term>, those which are most closely related to <term>perfection</term> can be [transformed] more easily, quickly, better, and more perfectly...

<meta>catchword: ...ly (cher)</meta>

<summary>In this concluding section, Bacon explains the power of the red and white Elixirs to transmute base metals into gold and silver. He notes that while the Elixir works on all metals, the speed of transmutation depends on how close the base metal is to "perfection."</summary>

### SPECULUM ALCHEMIAE

...more easily brought there, than those that are far distant and deviated from it. Because we have a metal that is more closely and better related to *perfection* <note>original: *perfection*; in alchemical theory, all metals "strive" to become gold, which is considered the only perfect metal</note>, which Nature has given to us, we do not need to seek our success in those that are situated so far away. Which metals are the nearest and the furthest, and which are closest to completeness, and which are furthest removed from it, has been previously indicated by me, and sufficiently revealed and made known. 

You can certainly recognize and learn this, provided your intellect is still sound and you know how to reflect upon these matters. Therefore, have no doubt: whoever has looked into and examined himself in this "Mirror" of mine in such a way, and has clearly grasped everything so that he has found the *Matter* <note>original: *Materiam*; the raw substance or "First Matter" used to begin the Great Work</note>, will also be able to understand upon which *Body* <note>original: *Corpus*; the physical base metal (like lead or mercury) that serves as the host for the transmutation</note> he should cast this *Medicine* <note>original: *Medicin*; the Philosopher's Stone in its active, healing form</note> so that he may bring it to *perfection*. For our former ancient teachers of this art, who discovered it through *Philosophy* <note>original: *Philosophey*; the study of natural laws and the hidden properties of matter</note>, point almost with their fingers to the correct and uncovered path when they say: Nature illuminates...

<summary>Bacon explains that while the Elixir can transform any metal, a wise alchemist chooses a "Body" or base metal already close to perfection to ensure a faster and more successful transmutation. He concludes by affirming that those who truly understand his "Mirror" will know exactly how to perform the final Projection.</summary>

### OF ROGER BACON

and understands Nature: Nature surpasses Nature: And Nature meets its own nature and rejoices in it/ and is transformed into another nature. <note>This is a famous alchemical axiom often attributed to pseudo-Democritus, expressing the idea that the internal forces of a substance are what drive its transformation.</note> And in another place: Like associates with its like. For similarity is a cause of love and friendship: regarding which many excellent *philosophers* <note>original: *Philosophi*; referring to the alchemists and natural scientists of antiquity</note> have left behind a memorable secret. You should also know that every *soul* <note>original: *Seele*; in alchemy, the "soul" is often the volatile spirit or vapor that is released during heating</note> enters its *body* <note>original: *Leib*; the solid, physical residue or "fixed" matter</note> quickly and with longing/ which does not allow itself to be united with a foreign body at all. And furthermore, in another place it is written: The soul enters its own body very quickly/ but if you wished to bind and unite it with a foreign body/ you would undertake such a thing in vain/ for the affinity and similarity is much brighter and clearer. So at last, in the completion of the *work*... <note>The "Great Work" (*Magnum Opus*) refers to the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone through repeated cycles of distillation and solidification.</note>

D
<meta>catchword: ...the work [that]</meta>

<summary>Bacon explains the fundamental alchemical principle that 'like attracts like,' arguing that a substance's volatile spirit (soul) can only truly unite with its own purified solid matter (body) to achieve transformation. This cycle of turning solids into spirits and spirits back into solids is the core mechanism of the alchemical process.</summary>

<meta>This page is a preface written by the editor, Joachim Tanckius, introducing the specific alchemical works of Roger Bacon. It follows the conclusion of the previous text and serves to frame the importance of the gold-based tincture as the foundation for all subsequent alchemical operations involving vitriol and antimony.</meta>

# To the Favorable
## Reader.

Among the <term>tinctures</term> <note>original German: *Tincturen*; in alchemy, these are potent liquids or substances capable of transforming base metals into gold or curing illnesses.</note> of which Roger Bacon, an English philosopher and alchemist, has written, the most noble is that which takes its root from gold. For this reason, I have chosen to place it first, considering that the others, which are prepared from <term>vitriol</term> <note>A sulfate mineral used to create powerful acids for dissolving metals.</note> and <term>antimony</term> <note>A brittle, silvery-white metalloid used in the purification of gold.</note>, are directed toward this one. For not only the <term>theory</term> <note>original Latin: *Theorica*</note> but also the <term>practice</term> <note>original Latin: *Practica*</note> provides a better understanding and instruction of the same. Thus, this work is presented and preferred before the others as a foundation and ground. And just as he points toward vitriol in his work on antimony, so both treatises look toward this one, and each explains the other.

However, I wish to remind the lover of this art that he should first learn to understand with all diligence what is presented to us in *The Mirror of Alchemy* <note>original Latin: *Speculo Alchimiæ*; one of the most famous medieval alchemical texts attributed to Roger Bacon.</note>. For within it, the key to the entire art is contained briefly, yet clearly enough. Now, whoever wishes to read these treatises with profit must compare one with the other; then everything will be easy and of little difficulty for the person of understanding. For an ass will hardly learn to fly, even if you should make wings for him. Farewell.

Joachim Tanckius, <term>Doctor</term> <note>The "D." following the name is an abbreviation for Doctor, signifying Tanckius's status as a learned physician and professor.</note>

<summary>In this preface, editor Joachim Tanckius introduces Roger Bacon's gold-based alchemical tincture as the essential foundation for understanding more complex operations. He advises the reader to study Bacon's "Mirror of Alchemy" as a prerequisite for success in the art.</summary>
<keywords>Joachim Tanckius, Roger Bacon, Mirror of Alchemy, gold tincture, vitriol, antimony, alchemical theory and practice</keywords>

<margin>
*Art emulates Nature.* <note>original Latin: *Ars Natura emula*.</note>

**E**very one of the ancient, true, and rightful philosophers and <term>naturalists</term> <note>original German: *Naturkündiger*; those who study the secrets and workings of the natural world.</note> has said: *Where Nature ends, there ART begins.* <note>original Latin: *Vbi Natura definit, ibi ARS incipit.* This is a fundamental alchemical maxim suggesting that human skill (Art) can refine and perfect what nature has only partially formed.</note> This glorious and excellent <term>aphorism</term> <note>A concise statement of a scientific principle or philosophical truth.</note> was truly not spoken into the wind or recorded by them without purpose, but was instead spoken and recorded very wisely and is well worth considering. For how could they have described and indicated the perfectly philosophical matters—both the <term>Particulars</term> <note>Processes aimed at specific results, such as transmuting a specific amount of base metal into silver or gold.</note> and the <term>Universals</term> <note>The pursuit of the "Universal Medicine" or Philosopher's Stone that can perfect all things.</note>—more briefly, more properly, or indeed more certainly and understandably? To interpret and explain this rich saying at length and according to its full content, it would be rightly judged appropriate to fill several books of paper with writing.

All things strive for perfection. <note>original Latin: *Omnia perfectionem appetunt*.</note>

They become **salt**, though distinct in each case. For one goes into the substance of **common salt** <note>original Latin: *salis communis*.</note>, another into **salt of vitriol** <note>original Latin: *Sal vitrioli*; likely iron or copper sulfate.</note>, the third into **saltpeter** <note>original Latin: *Nitrum*.</note>, and the like.

</margin>

One goes into the substance and nature of <term>Jupiter</term> <note>original Latin: *Iovis*; the alchemical name for tin.</note>, another into the substance of <term>Venus</term> <note>original Latin: *Veneris*; the alchemical name for copper.</note>, and the third into the nature of <term>the Moon</term> <note>original Latin: *Lunam*; silver.</note>, <term>Mars</term> <note>original Latin: *Martis*; iron.</note>, and <term>the Sun</term> <note>original Latin: *Solis*; gold.</note>.

# Tincture of the Sun <note>original: *Tinctur Solis*; in alchemy, the "Sun" or "Sol" refers to Gold, and a "Tincture" is a transformative substance.</note>

...changes the <term>nature</term> <note>original: *Natur*.</note> and <term>substance</term> <note>original: *Substanz*.</note> of lead, etc. Just as lead on rooftops—through the moisture and <term>warmth</term> <note>original: *Werme*.</note> of the Sun—passes into the <term>substance</term> and essence of silver, so too, out of <term>nature</term> and its progression, all other <term>imperfect bodies</term> <note>original Latin: *imperfecta Corpora*.</note> of the metals may be transformed into the <term>substance of the Sun</term> <note>original Latin: *substantiam Solis*; Gold.</note>.

Nothing is more perfect than the Sun or Gold. <note>original Latin: *Sole seu auro nihil est perfectius*.</note>
</margin>
But here we learn of <term>nature's</term> ultimate goal: for never has anything been discovered that surpasses such a <term>perfect</term> <note>original: *perfectes*.</note> being and <term>nature</term> as that of gold. Indeed, it possesses such uniformity with the elements that it stands equal to them in all things.

<margin>
Gold is more perfect than gemstones. Why gemstones are imperfect. <note>original Latin: *Aurum perfectius est gemmis Cur gemmæ imperfectæ*.</note>
</margin>
Many have indeed claimed that precious stones surpass gold, but they have erred. For in gemstones there is no <term>perfection</term> <note>original: *perfection*.</note> nor <term>equality</term> <note>original: *aqualitet*; a balanced proportion</note> of all four elements. In fact, a single one of the elements could completely and utterly destroy them. Even if they show themselves to be wondrous, and even if it appears as though they were worth more and were more powerful than gold, they are simply not.

### Concerning Theory

<margin>
The perfection of gold; the imperfection of gems. <note>original Latin: *Auri perfectio. Gemmarū imperfectio*.</note>
</margin>
...is, since Gold possesses a union and is uniform with all four elements. And the most noble stone is governed solely by the <term>Star</term> <note>original: *Astro*; in alchemical philosophy, this refers to the celestial influence or "firmament" that grants a substance its specific virtues.</note>, and when its <term>term</term> <note>original: *Terminus*; the allotted time or boundary of its natural power.</note> is finished, the stone—which the Star made noble—is now as a common pebble, or something similar.

<margin>
Gold, being more perfect than gems, can never be corrupted. <note>original Latin: *Aurum perfectius gemmis nunquam corrumpi potest*.</note>
</margin>
This truly does not happen to gold; for until the <term>Final Judgment</term> <note>original: *letzten Vrtheil des Gerichts*; the author suggests that gold is ontologically stable until the end of the world.</note>, nature can neither change nor diminish anything of the <term>nobility</term> <note>original: *nobilitet*.</note> and <term>perfection</term> <note>original: *perfection*.</note> of the body of gold. It is also indeed a <term>Matter</term> <note>original: *Materia*.</note> of all precious stones, and yields them better from its own body and its matter than nature can otherwise find or reach. And I tell you, if the <term>Star</term> should turn and <term>impress</term> <note>original: *imprimiren*; to stamp or fix an influence into a substance.</note> its <term>inclination</term> <note>original: *inclination*; the specific celestial direction or tendency.</note> into such a <term>clarified</term> <note>original: *clarificirten*; purified or made radiant.</note> body of gold, it could not lose its <term>virtue</term> <note>original Latin: *Virtutem*; the inherent healing or transformative power.</note> and <term>power</term> <note>original Latin: *Potens*.</note> until the Final Judgment. For the body is <term>perfect</term> and united with all elements and agreeable to them, and there is no element that might harm it.

# PRACTICE

<margin>Dissolution into the first matter or Mercury. <note>original: *Solutio in primam materiam seu Mercurium*.</note></margin>
The **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers to alchemists—those who study the hidden laws of nature.</note> desire that the first **dissolution** <note>original: *solutio*. The process of breaking down a solid into a liquid state.</note> of our Gold should occur into the **First Matter** <term>First Matter: *Prima materia*, the formless, fundamental substance from which all things are created, believed by alchemists to be the starting point of the Great Work.</term>, namely brought into **living Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium vivum*. This refers to quicksilver (mercury) in a highly purified or "animated" state.</note>, for it has come from and was born from a Mercury.

<margin>A comparison from the freezing of water. <note>original: *Simile a coagulatione Aquae*.</note></margin>
For they say: just as ice is **coagulated** and frozen from water by the cold, so its first dissolution is back into a water.

And as this example of ice indicates, demonstrates, and provides us to understand: so also should **Gold**—which was Mercury in the first place—be brought back to that same state in its first dissolution, namely into Mercury; that is, into **living Mercury**, or **running Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium currentem*. A term for liquid quicksilver that flows easily.</note>.

Therefore, it will be necessary that we indicate something concerning the **body of the sun** <note>original: *leibe solis*. In the symbolic language of alchemy, the "Sun" (Sol) always refers to Gold.</note> and introduce **philosophically** the method, or by what means such a breaking or dissolution of the body might be possible and could happen naturally. For the Philosophers say: "It is easier to make gold than to..." <note>original: *Facilius est aurum facere, quàm...* The author is quoting a common alchemical maxim suggesting that while creating gold is difficult, truly destroying or "opening" its perfect structure is even harder.</note>

# PRACTICAL APPLICATION <note>original: *PRACTICA*. This section moves from theory to the specific methods of the alchemical work.</note>

<margin>For rotting, first, there is a need for moisture. <note>original: *Ad putrefactionem 1. opus est Humido*.</note></margin>
Now, however, you should further know three other things: that rotting <note>original: *Putrefactio*. In alchemy, this refers to the decomposition of a substance to its primary state.</note> cannot happen without moistening. For its greatest power resides in moisture, as can be observed in the fruits and seeds of the field.

<margin>Second, there is also a need for a corrosive agent. <note>original: *2. Opus est quoque Corrosivo*.</note></margin>
Secondly, rotting also requires corrosion. For such a corrosive process <note>original: *Corrosio*.</note> must follow after the moistening. Without this corrosive agent—whether it is added to the substance or the substance creates it within itself—no perfect rotting can occur. We see examples of this in every natural birth. For what brings the grain in the field to its state of rotting, if not a corrosive agent? <note>The author views the enzymes or soil acids that break down a seed as "corrosives."</note> Nothing. For the corrosive must take over the body, dismantle it entirely, drive its parts away from one another, and unlock and destroy <note>original: *destruiren*.</note> it down to the smallest point.

Manure, however, is nothing other than <term>saltpeter</term><note>Potassium nitrate, a salt used in alchemy and for making gunpowder, often found in animal waste.</note> mixed with other salts. For one may boil precious saltpeter from sheep manure, as experience demonstrates. Likewise, the manure from other **animals**, such as chickens and pigeons, yields a very burning and sharp salt.

<margin>The method of dissolution. <note>original: *Solutionis modus*.</note></margin>
Now, when the seed is cast into such salty, moist earth, the salt first begins to dissolve <note>original: *resolviren*. To break down or melt into a liquid state.</note> and breaks the **physical body** <note>original: *Corpus*. The material substance of the matter being worked upon.</note> apart, after which a perfect **rotting** may occur.

<margin>All things are preserved by their own salt. <note>original: *Omnia sale suo conservantur*.</note></margin>
But you should also know this: that no sap or water lacks such a salt, even though it is often not tasted by the mouth or tongue, nor visibly seen.

## The Second Chapter:
# On Sublimation.
<note>original: *De Sublimatione*; the process of heating a substance into a vapor so it collects as a solid on a cooler surface, used here metaphorically and chemically to "lift" the spirit from the matter.</note>

<margin>
1. There is one dissolution of the body and its parts.
There is another dissolution of the Soul and the spirit.
<note>original: *Solutio alia est corporis & partium eius. Alia est Animae & spiritus solutio.*</note>
</margin>
Now that we have prepared our **Material** <note>original: *Materiam*; the raw substance or "Prime Matter" being worked upon</note> according to the **Philosophers** <note>A term for alchemists of the past whose writings served as the foundation for the "Art"</note> and their teachings, we rightfully proceed to the **second mode** <note>original: *modo*; the specific method or stage of operation</note>, namely to the **second key** of our **Work**. Here we are informed of another **Heavenly opening** <note>original: *Himlischen auffschliessung*; likely referring to a more volatile or "spiritual" chemical breakdown compared to the previous stage</note>, specifically that of the **Soul** and the **Spirit**.

<margin>
Anatomy of the Soul and the Spirit.
<note>original: *Anatomia Animae & Spiritus*</note>
</margin>
For the first opening was only an opening of the **Body** and its members. This opening, however—or rather, this dissolution and dissection—serves for an **Essential Anatomy** <note>An "essential" anatomy refers to the study of the inner, fundamental nature of the substance rather than its outward physical form</note> of both the **Spirit** and the **Soul**.

<margin>
Why the Spirit retains the Soul.
<note>original: *Cur Animae quam spiritus retinet*</note>
</margin>
Why of the **Spirit**? Because the Spirit holds within it the hidden **Soul** of our Body, which all the **Philosophers** have sought, and few among them have been able to find.

For what reason? Because she is

# TINCTURES OF THE GOLD <note>original Latin: *Tincturae Solis*</note>

<margin>
The **force and power** <note>original Latin: *vis & potentia*</note> of the **Stone** <note>original Latin: *Lapidis*</note>.
</margin>
...of the spirit, just as a magnet <note>original: *Magneten*</note> attacks iron with its power and compels it to itself: And how a very small **quantity** <note>original Latin: *quantitet*</note> of the **Philosophical Stone** <note>original Latin: *Philosophischen Lapidis*</note> draws a great amount of **common Mercury** <note>original Latin: *Mercurij vulgi*</note> to itself and coagulates <note>thickens or solidifies</note> it into a **perfect Body of the Sun or Moon** <note>original Latin: *perfect Corpus Solis vel Lunae*; gold or silver</note>.

And how the Magical **Super-celestial** <note>original: *Supercaelestische*; referring to a spiritual or higher-plane reality</note> Stone, solely through its brilliance and gaze, creates a **transmutation** <term>Transmutation; the alchemical transformation of one element into another</term> of the metals. And how the **Basilisk** <note>a legendary reptile said to kill with a single glance</note> kills people through the gleam of its eyes: And how the **Conjunctions** <note>original Latin: *Coniunctiones*; the alignment of planets or stars</note> of the heavenly bodies, through their appearance, stir up and cause war, pestilence, and crop failure: 

So, and in the same way, it is also with our Soul; when it is in its highest **Ascent** <note>original Latin: *Ascensu*</note> and **Exaltation** <term>Exaltation; the process of refining a substance to its highest possible potency</term>, it **inspires** <note>original: *inspirirt*; literally "breathes into"</note> the spirit, through its strength of power, to become **physically perfect** <note>original: *Corporalisch perfect*</note> and indestructible once again. For just as such a power of **sulfur** <note>original Latin: *sulphuris*</note> within the **substance** of **Mercury** is driven toward the **Perfection** of Gold—so should you be informed of nothing other than a similar process here, though one of a higher power and form.

# TINCTURES OF THE SUN <note>original Latin: *Tincturae Solis*</note>

Since it also, from the very beginning, originates from the first **Matter** <term>Hyle: a Greek term used by alchemists to refer to the "primordial matter" or the raw, unformed substance of the universe</term>, in which Art <note>In this context, "Art" refers to the practice of Alchemy itself</note> has placed it, it brings us such fruits as if they were more than mere elements.

<margin>I. Dissolution of the Sun <note>original Latin: *Solutio Solis*</note></margin>
Indeed, it has also been broken open and unlocked into its innermost core through the mineral spirits of **sal ammoniac**, **saltpeter**, and **alkali**, which did not cease until they brought forth to our eyes and delivered into the light the promised **Heavenly Matter** <note>original: *Hyle*</note> of this substance of ours.

<margin>The author’s prophecy <note>original Latin: *vaticinium autoris*</note></margin>
Truly, so that we do not deny such things or dismiss them contemptuously as if to the wind, the time that is to come after us shall help to testify this publicly among our own people.

<term>Vitriol: A historical term for sulfate minerals (like iron or copper sulfate). In alchemy, "Oil of Vitriol" usually referred to concentrated sulfuric acid, considered a primary agent for transmuting metals and creating medicines.</term>
### BY ROGER BACON, THE ENGLISHMAN.
<note>Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292) was a Franciscan friar and philosopher. While he did write on science and alchemy, many later "Baconian" alchemical texts like this one were likely attributed to him to lend them authority.</note>

# To the Art-Loving / Reader.

Gracious reader, this precious treatise on Vitriol—explaining how one should prepare and arrange through art and special <term>skill: The original German "Handgriff" implies specific manual techniques or "knacks" of the laboratory.</term> a magnificent and useful oil and medicine, serving for the healing of many severe illnesses that afflict mankind, and also how metals may thereby be improved—has come to me from a good friend in the *original manuscript of Doctor Ortolph Marold, late Physician to the Prince of Henneberg, most highly skilled in the chemical art.* <note>original Latin/German: "autographo Doctoris Ortolphi Maroldi, weyland Fürstlichen Hennbergischen Medici, in arte Chemica peritissimi." Ortolph Marold (1569–1606) was a respected physician and contemporary of the editor, Joachim Tancke.</note> Since nothing of the kind concerning Vitriol has ever been published in print, I felt I should not separate it from the other small treatises of *Roger Bacon*, for the sake of the common good.
->Farewell.<- <note>original Latin: "Vale."</note>

<image-desc>A small decorative printer's ornament featuring symmetrical stylized foliage and scrollwork.</image-desc>

<summary>In this preface, the editor explains that he received a rare manuscript about the medicinal and metallurgical uses of vitriol, originally owned by the physician Ortolph Marold. He has chosen to publish it alongside Roger Bacon's works because of its unique value and previous unavailability in print.</summary>

<margin>The dignity and excellence of Vitriol among Philosophers.</margin>

# ON THE OIL

<margin>The initial letters denote the word Vitriol.</margin>
Several sayings, maxims, and rhymes have been composed, just as they have also done and said regarding Vitriol: *Visit the interior of the earth; by rectifying, you will find the hidden stone, the true Medicine.* <note>original Latin: "Visitetis interiora terræ rectificando invenietis occultum lapidem, veram Medicinam." This is the famous "V.I.T.R.I.O.L." acronym, a central mnemonic in alchemy. "Rectifying" refers to the process of purifying a substance through distillation or refinement.</note>

<margin>Explanation of the saying.</margin>
The words themselves provide the understanding, which we explain thus: While the ancients say that one should visit the earth with rectification, this is not to be understood as

<margin>The Earth is Vitriol.</margin>
the base, common earth from somewhere in the mountains or the wilderness, but rather the "earth" of Vitriol.

<margin>The interior of Vitriol.</margin>
<margin>By sight.</margin>
Further they say: That the interior parts of the earth should be visited, and not the exterior. For from the outside, Vitriol—as the eyes see it—is green in color, <note>Iron sulfate, or green vitriol, appears as translucent green crystals.</note> but inside, the philosophers find it red. <note>In alchemical theory, the "redness" hidden within the green exterior represented the soul or the mature potency of the mineral, often revealed through heat (calcination).</note>

<margin>By taste.</margin>
The tongue tastes it from the outside as sharp and bitter, yet inside it is sweet and pleasant.

<margin>By touch.</margin>
The hands touch it on the outside as hard, rough, coarse, sharp, and impure; yet inside it is tender and lovely, being quite a subtle, noble juice and balsam, and a pure oil.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<vocab>Vitriol, Alchemy, Visit the interior of the earth, Medicine, Philosopher's Stone, Rectification, Oil</vocab>

It describes the sensory contradictions of Vitriol—green and bitter on the outside, but red, sweet, and oily on the inside—as a metaphor for hidden spiritual or medicinal potency.</summary>

# OF VITRIOL

<margin>By smell.</margin>
The nose perceives its scent as quite wild and crude, like sulfur; yet in its innermost part, it is more noble and pleasant than ambergris or balsam. <note>Ambergris and balsam were highly prized, sweet-smelling substances used in perfumes and medicine, contrasted here with the "wild" smell of raw minerals like sulfur.</note>

<margin>Vain works in the preparation of Vitriol.</margin>
Although many philosophers have perhaps dreamed—or noticed from other signs of nature—that great secrets exist within Vitriol, they have searched for it for a long time, wishing to share in its hidden nature and mystery. But they would not understand that neither by distilling nor <term>calcining</term> <note>Calcination is the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder or "calx," a common but often destructive alchemical practice.</note> as they were accustomed to doing, could anything useful be produced; though some achieved more than others.

<margin>Improper calcination of vitriol.</margin>
Now, however, the correct process is not to calcine it. For as soon as it is deprived of its green color, the oil and the spirit of the <term>Arcatur</term> <note>original: "Arcatur." Likely a derivation of "Arcanum," referring to the secret transformative power or the "hidden spirit" of the substance.</note> of all true philosophers is already lost.

# ->ON OIL<-
<note>original: "DE OLEO." In alchemy, "oil" often refers to the concentrated, fatty, or combustible essence of a substance, not just common cooking oil.</note>

...one may see how the **Elements** in **Nature** begin to **rectify** <term>The process of purifying a liquid—often through repeated distillation, aging, or filtering—to reach its most potent and "upright" state.</term> the mere, base water. For in several days it becomes cloudy and stinking, and it deposits its **dregs** <note>original: "faces"</note>—coarse, earthly, and corruptible impurity—at the bottom. Decant the pure part of it carefully, so that such coarse impurity does not go along with it; seal it up again and let it stand in the air for several days. Then the water will again deposit many **dregs**, even more than before, and it will smell foul. Do to this again as was taught before, drawing it off from its **dregs** <note>original: "facibus"</note> and sealing it up again, so that it is preserved in the air. # OF THE OIL <note>original: *DE OLIO*</note>

<margin>Red oil <note>original: *Oleum rubeum*</note></margin>

## The Projection of our Medicine.

<margin>Projection <note>original: *Proiectio*; the final stage of the Great Work, where a small amount of the "Medicine" is cast onto molten base metal to transmute it into gold or silver</note></margin>
It is not the least among matters to perform the projection of the Medicine upon the metals, as many have rightly intended. For when the metals are coarse and impure, it brings great harm to the master craftsman <note>original: *Werckmeister*</note>. For this reason, <margin>Concerning the impurity of imperfect metals <note>original: *De metallorum imperfectorum impuritate*</note></margin> the Medicine remains partly stuck in the dross <note>original: *Schlacken*; the stony waste or scum separated from metals during smelting</note> and does not touch the pure part of the metal. Therefore, I deem it proper to provide a brief report on this matter here, so that one does not bring the noble Medicine to naught and uselessly throw it away with the dross. And you should know that all bodies of imperfect metals <note>original: *imperfecten Metallen*; base metals like lead, tin, or copper that the alchemist seeks to "perfect" into gold</note> are so impure that they are also with difficulty, some...

<meta>catchword: the.</meta>

<margin>False opinions concerning Tinctures <note>original: *Falsæ opiniones de Tincturis*</note></margin>
Several have indeed claimed—though falsely and from a fabricated basis—that one may tincture <note>original: *tingiren*; to color or transform the surface or essence of a metal</note> the metals through the oils and waters of the Tinctures so that they remain entirely whole. Some lie even more regarding this, saying they remain malleable. I once encountered such a liar who said he had smeared a silver groschen <note>original: *Groschen*; a common silver coin</note> with his Tincture, and the entire groschen was transmuted into a malleable gold without losing its minted image <note>original: *Gepregs*; the stamped design on the coin</note>. When I told him, however, that it could not be true and was contrary to nature and its essence, he began to swear and bluster <note>original: *schnarchen*; literally "to snore," used here to mean huffing with indignation or bragging loudly</note>. But when it came time for the proof, there was nothing to it at all.

### OF VITRIOL <note>original: *Vitrioli*</note>

Therefore, my son, you should know that when one applies the Medicine, the Oils <note>original: *Olea*; refined alchemical extracts</note>, upon an imperfect body <note>original: *onvollkommenen Leichnam*; a term for base metals like iron or copper that were considered "dead" compared to gold</note>, it falls apart completely and breaks like clay. <meta>This page continues the practical instructions for the "Projection," the final stage of the alchemical work where the transformative substance (the Medicine) is added to base metals. The previous page discussed the hierarchy of metals suitable for this process, ending with the importance of the quality of the material.</meta>

### OF VITRIOL <note>original: *VITRIOLI*; vitriol refers to sulfate minerals, often used as a starting material for creating alchemical acids and "oils."</note>

If too much [of the Medicine] is cast forth, it becomes brittle; then one should add more of the **imperfect metal** <note>base metals like lead or copper that the alchemist seeks to "perfect" into gold or silver</note> to it. But if there is too little **Medicine** <note>the refined alchemical substance used for transformation</note> in the **Projection** <note>the act of casting the alchemist's stone or oil onto molten metal</note>, then observe the weight of the **Medicine** in its casting, and refine it upon the **cupel** <note>original: *test*; a shallow, porous dish used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals through intense heat</note>. Then you may know for certain how many parts your **Medicine** **tinges** <note>original: *tingiret*; the alchemical power to "color" or transform a specific number of parts of base metal into gold or silver</note>. Likewise, one **Venus** <note>the alchemical name for copper</note> is often purer than another; therefore, the **Projection** must also turn out differently. Yet you should note that it is best if **Venus** and other **metals** are first purified, and that you perform the **Projection** thereafter. It should also occur in **flux** <note>original: *Fluß*; the molten, liquid state of metal under heat</note> while the metal is driven by the heat, for in this way it settles all the purer.

Whatever else is necessary, you might seek in that place, in the ***Treatise on Projection*** <note>original: *Tractat de Projectione*</note>; there everything is explained more extensively and with understanding. Instruction is also given there concerning **juices** <note>original: *Säfften*; liquid alchemical extracts or essences</note>, **oils**, **powders**, **stones**, and the like, with wonderful investigation into the greatest secrets.

<summary>This page provides instructions on calibrating the "Medicine" during the transmutation process, noting that the purity of the base metal (Venus/copper) affects the outcome. It emphasizes performing the projection while the metal is molten and refers the reader to a specialized treatise for deeper secrets.</summary>
<keywords>Projection, Medicine, Venus, copper, cupellation, purification, transmutation, flux, Treatise on Projection, alchemical secrets</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page concludes the chapter on Vitriol, following the previous discussion on the process of Projection (the transformation of metals). It serves as a transition, pointing the reader toward the next stage of the work found in a separate treatise on Antimony.</meta>

### ON THE OIL OF VITRIOL <note>original: *DE OLEO VITRIOLI*; vitriol refers to metallic sulfate minerals, highly valued in alchemy for producing potent acids and oils.</note>

Now we conclude this chapter concerning the fifth mineral, **vitriol**, to the honor and praise of God and in service to our neighbor.

Regarding the **multiplication** <note>original: *Multiplication*; the alchemical process of increasing the quantity or the transmutative power of the "Medicine" or Philosopher's Stone</note>, you should search for it in the **treatise** <note>original: *Tractatu*</note> concerning **antimony** <note>original: *Antimonio*</note> or **stibnite** <note>original: *Stibio*; the natural sulfide mineral of antimony, often used to purify gold</note> and its oil. There you will find the account, for the time and the **method** <note>original: *Modus*</note> are one and the same; provided only that you reflect upon these matters with diligence and investigate nature.

->*The End of the Treatise on Vitriol.*<-

# Preface.
<sig>D 2</sig>

Dear Reader / **ROGER BACON** mentions at the end of the treatise on Vitriol / that regarding the *multiplication* <term>Multiplication: The alchemical process of increasing the potency or quantity of a transmuting substance.</term> of the tinctures made from vitriol / the lover of the Art should seek further instruction from the treatise *On the Oil of Antimony* <note>original Latin: *De Oleo Stibii*.</note>. For this reason, it has been considered good and useful / that the treatise *On the Oil of Antimony* follow immediately here / and be included. And when one well considers these tinctures / and *compares* <note>original: *conferiren*; to cross-reference or bring together for study.</note> them with one another / I have no doubt / it will not pass without special benefit. Yet it behooves every lover of the Art / that he direct one eye toward Nature at all times / and the other toward Art and manual work <note>original: *Handarbeit*; this refers to the practical laboratory labor required of the alchemist.</note>. For if these two do not stand together / it is a lame work / Just as if one thought / to walk a long distance on one foot / which would be impossible for him.

->*Farewell.* <note>original Latin: *Vale.*</note><-

**Joachim Tanckius, Doctor.** <note>Joachim Tanckius (1557–1609) was a professor of medicine at the University of Leipzig and a prolific editor of alchemical texts.</note>

<summary>In this preface, editor Joachim Tanckius explains that he has included Roger Bacon’s treatise on Antimony as a necessary companion to the previous work on Vitriol. He emphasizes that successful alchemy requires a balance between observing the laws of Nature and the physical labor of the "Art."</summary>
<keywords>Joachim Tanckius, Roger Bacon, Antimony, Vitriol, multiplication, Nature vs. Art, laboratory practice, alchemy preface</keywords>

# ON THE OIL

Indeed, some say that when one prepares **antimony** <note>original: *Stibium*.</note> into glass <note>The "Glass of Antimony" was a well-known alchemical preparation made by roasting the ore to an ash and then melting it into a transparent, reddish-brown glass.</note>, the evil volatile **sulfur** <note>original: *sulphur*; in alchemy, sulfur often represents the soul or the active principle, but when "volatile," it was seen as unstable or impure.</note> would depart from it, and the oil that could be prepared from that glass would be a very **fixed** <term>Fixed: A substance that is stable in heat and does not evaporate or change state when exposed to fire.</term> oil. They claim it would then truly provide an **ingress** <term>Ingress: The ability of an alchemical medicine or tincture to penetrate and permeate a base metal to transform it.</term> and **medicine** to bring imperfect metals to **perfection** <note>original: *perfection*; the state of being transformed into gold or silver.</note>.

These words and opinions are well-intended and seem correct, but that it should be so in reality, and prove itself to be the case—that will not happen. Take these **ingredients** <note>original: *species*; the individual components of the recipe</note> and mix them well together, and **distill** <note>original: *destilliret*; the process of heating and cooling to purify a liquid</note> a water from them, starting quite slowly at first. Once you have the dissolved **antimony** <note>original: *Antimonium*</note> pure and well **sweetened** <note>original: *abgesüsset*; the chemical process of washing away acids or salts with water until the residue is neutral or "sweet"</note>, and have washed it thoroughly of its sharp waters so that you no longer perceive any sharpness, then place it into a clean **vial** <note>original: *Viol*; a glass vessel or flask</note> and pour good distilled vinegar over it. Place the vial in **horse dung** <note>original: *fimum Equinum*; used as a source of gentle, steady heat in alchemy</note> or a **water bath** <note>original: *Balneum Maria*; literally "Mary's Bath," a double-boiler used for gentle heating</note> to **putrefy** <note>original: *putrificiren*; to let a substance decompose or digest under gentle heat</note> for forty days and nights (**others** <note>original: *alij*</note> say four). It will then **dissolve** and extract itself into a blood-red color.

<meta>This page continues the instructions for extracting the "oil" or essence of antimony. Having previously putrefied the antimony in a water bath or horse dung, the text now describes the repeated process of dissolving the material in vinegar, filtering it, and washing away the acidity.</meta>

### OF ANTIMONY
<note>original: *ANTIMONII*</note>

cleanly off into a **glass flask** <note>original: *Kolbenglaß*; a round-bottomed vessel with a long neck</note>, and pour other vinegar over it again. Place it into **digestion** <note>original: *Digestion*; a process of slow, gentle heating used to soften or dissolve substances</note> as before, so that if anything remains in the **dregs** <note>original: *fæcibus*; the solid residue or sediment</note>, it may also be **dissolved** <note>original: *solviren*</note>. For whatever good **substance** <note>original: *Substanz*</note> still remains among the dregs will certainly be able to dissolve during this time. Then, discard the **dregs**; they are no longer of any use, other than to be poured out onto the ground and thrown away.

<margin>Washing of the matter</margin> <note>original: *Lotio materiæ*</note>
Afterward, gather all the **solutions** <note>original: *solutiones*</note> together, poured into a glass flask in a **water bath** <note>original: *Balneum Mariæ*; a double-boiler used for gentle, uniform heating</note>, and **distill** <note>original: *destilliret*</note> all the sharp vinegar away from it again. Pour it back on, or use fresh vinegar if the former should become too weak; in this way, the **matter** <note>original: *Materi*</note> will dissolve very quickly in the vinegar. Distill it away once more so that the matter remains entirely dry. Then, take common distilled water and wash away all the sharpness which remains from the vinegar...

# OF THE OIL
<note>original: *DE OLIO*</note>

But now we wish to hasten to the other work of the **Tincture** <note>A potent alchemical liquid capable of transmuting metals or healing the body</note> and provide a report on it. Take the mixture of the **oil** and **spirit of wine**, put it into a **flask** <note>original: *Kolben*; a round-bottomed glass vessel used for boiling</note>, place a **distillation head** <note>original: *Helm*; the upper part of the still that directs vapors downward</note> upon it, provide a **receiver** <note>original: *Vorlage*; the vessel that collects the condensed liquid</note>, and place them together in the **Water Bath** <note>original: *Balneum Mariæ*; literally "Mary's Bath," a vessel of hot water used to heat the apparatus gently and steadily</note>. Distill all the **spirit of wine** away from the **oil** with entirely gentle heat, so that you are certain that no more **spirit of wine** remains beneath the very precious **oil**. And this can be easily tested: when you see certain drops rising with the **spirit of wine** over the **distillation head** and falling into the **receiver**, that is the sign that the **spirit of wine** has been separated from the **oil**. Then remove all the fire from under the **Water Bath**, no matter how little of it there may be, so that it may cool down all the sooner.

## For Gout or Aches <note>original: *Podagra* and *Zipperlein*; historical terms for gout and rheumatic pain</note>.

## In Apoplexy <note>term: *Apoplexia*; a sudden loss of consciousness or paralysis, commonly known as a stroke</note> or Stroke.

In the event of a stroke, let one drop—pure and undiluted—fall upon the tongue of the person in need. This will immediately rise and disperse like a mist and smoke, and it will restore and resolve that which was stricken. However, if the stroke has affected the body or other limbs, give the patient three drops at once in good wine, according to the instructions previously given for Gout <note>term: *Podagra*; a painful form of inflammatory arthritis</note>.

## In Dropsy <note>term: *Hydrops*; an old medical term for the swelling of body tissues due to excess fluid, now called edema</note>.

For dropsy, give one drop each time for six consecutive days in Lemon Balm water <note>original: *Aqua Melissæ*</note> or Valerian water <note>original: *Valerianæ*</note>. On the seventh day, give three drops in good wine; that is sufficient.

In the falling sickness <note>term: *fallende Sucht*; a historical name for epilepsy, describing the way patients fall during a seizure</note>, give the patient at the beginning of the paroxysm <note>term: *Paroxismi*; the sudden onset or fit of a disease</note> two drops in Sage water <note>original Latin: *Aqua Salvie*</note>, and after three hours, another two drops; this is quite sufficient for him. However, if the condition should stir again, give him two more drops, as has been stated.

## In Consumption <note>term: *Hectica* or *Schwindsucht*; a wasting disease or chronic fever often associated with tuberculosis</note>.

In cases of consumption and wasting away, give the patient two drops in Violet water <note>original Latin: *Aqua violarum*</note> on the first day; on the second day, give another two drops in good wine.

## In Fevers <note>original Latin: *Febribus*</note>.

In all kinds of hot fevers, give the patient at the beginning of the paroxysm <note>the onset of a fever spike or chill</note> three drops in a good distilled St. John’s wort water <note>original German: *Johanneskraut wasser*</note> or Chicory. The following day, early in the morning on an empty stomach, give another two drops in good wine.

### In the Plague <note>original Latin: *In Peste*</note>.

In the pestilence <note>the plague</note>, give to the afflicted <note>original German: *Dürfftigen*; meaning the one in need or the patient</note> seven drops in a good wine, and ensure that the infected person is entirely alone, and that they sweat well afterward; thus, such poison <note>referring to the plague-matter in the body</note>, through the granting of Divine help, shall not harm their life.

## For the Prolonging and Maintenance of a Healthy Life.

Take and give at the beginning and onset of Spring <note>term: *Lentzens*; an archaic German term for the springtime</note>, when the sun has entered the sign of Aries <note>term: *Widders*; the Ram, marking the spring equinox</note>, two drops; and at the beginning and onset of Autumn also two drops. Thus, everyone who uses it is, with God’s help, well-secured and protected against ill-health and poisoned air <note>a reference to "miasma," the historical belief that diseases were spread by foul vapors in the atmosphere</note>. This is provided that the oncoming illness was not predestined <note>original German: *praedestinirt*</note> and ordained for the person's end by God Almighty.

### Concerning the
# Multiplica-
# tion of the
## Stone of Antimony <note>original: *Lapidis Stibij*; "Stibium" is the Latin term for the mineral antimony</note>.

The ancient Sages, once they had discovered the Stone and prepared it to its perfect power and the transformation <note>original: *vbersetzung*; in this context, the alchemical "translation" or conversion of one substance into another</note> of imperfect metals into gold, researched for a long time whether a way could be found to increase the power and effect of such a stone. -> OF ANTIMONY <-

The second increase is an *increase in quantity* <note>original: *augmentum quantitatis*</note> of the Stone / in its previous power / so that it loses nothing of its power nor receives more / but indeed increases in its weight / and multiplies further and further / so that from one Loth <note>A traditional German unit of weight, approximately 14 to 16 grams or half an ounce</note> many Loths grow and increase.

Praise and thank God the Almighty for His high revelation /

<meta>This page is the Preface (Vorrede) to the "Epistle of Alexander," following the title page where Joachim Tanckius introduced the work. # Preface.

Favorable reader, this Epistle <note>original: *Epist-*; a formal letter or dispatch.</note> came to me under the name and title of the Emperor *Alexander*: and thus, just as I am giving it to you in print, it was already translated into German. As to which Emperor should be understood here—since many of that name have reigned in Macedonia—I leave that for you to judge. Whether you wish to attribute it to *Alexander the Great*, who otherwise held all the arts in high regard, or to another, that is for you to decide. I have only seen to it that, because the <term>Stone</term> <note>original: *Lapide*. Short for *Lapis Philosophorum* or the Philosopher’s Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold.</note> is treated within it in the <term>manner of a parable</term>, and the composition <note>original: *Getichte*; in this context, it refers to a symbolic story or allegorical narrative rather than a rhyming poem.</note> is not unrefined, it is well worth reading. Especially on account of the significance of the Masters who are summoned to make the King healthy again; with this, the art-loving reader shall be entertained and may further reflect upon the matter and to what it refers. For it is clearly to be gathered from this that this Epistle was written by a learned and experienced *Philosopher* and student of nature.

->*Farewell.*<- <note>original: *Vale.*</note>

<summary>In this preface, the editor Joachim Tanckius introduces a letter attributed to Emperor Alexander, debating its historical authorship while praising its alchemical merit. He highlights the text's use of a parable involving "Masters" healing a "King" as a sophisticated metaphor for the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

<keywords>Preface, Alexander the Great, Macedonia, Philosopher's Stone, alchemy, parable, healing the King, Joachim Tanckius, philosophy of nature</keywords>

The text establishes the Hermetic principle of the "as above, so below" relationship between the human being (microcosm) and the universe (macrocosm).</meta>

### Epistle
# Of the Emperor
## Alexander.

---

Some say briefly that the <term>Art</term> <note>original: *Die Kunst*; almost always refers to alchemy, the "Great Art" of transformation.</note> is taken from the creation of man, in whom all things are contained. Just as the <term>Small World</term> <note>original: *kleine Welt*; the Microcosm, a philosophical concept where the human being is viewed as a miniature map of the entire universe.</note> is composed and created from four distinct forces: So also is this Art [derived] from the nature of the **Woman** <note>original: *des Weibes Naturen*; in alchemy, the "feminine" typically represents the passive, moist, or mercurial principle that must be combined with the "masculine" or solar principle.</note>, of which we will briefly recount, prove, and establish several aspects.

As the <term>Great World</term> <note>original: *grosse Welt*; the Macrocosm, or the universe at large.</note> is created or composed of four distinct and gathered forces of nature, of which the first is the **Desiring** <note>original: *Begehrende*; likely referring to the *Attractive* faculty of nature.</note>, the second the **Breaking-down**— <note>original: *Abbewende*; this likely refers to the second of the four natural faculties, the *Digestive* or *Repelling* force. The text breaks off here at the catchword.</note>

### OF ALEXANDER.

...will explain these and their **Power** <term>Power</term> <note>original: *Macht*; refers to the inherent virtue or potency of a substance.</note>, and will say which is warm, cold, **Masculine**, and which is **Feminine**, so that we do not go astray through various colors and the distinction of their genders. And you should know that no one is born otherwise than from a man and a woman, and no **Fruit** grows otherwise than through **moisture** and **heat**.

Therefore it is fitting that we explain the **Power** <note>original: *Krafft*.</note>.

For the **Desiring** powers are dry and **Masculine**, and are warm and dry, and are the **Fire**.

The **Retaining** or "holding-to-themselves" powers are also **Masculine**, and are cold and dry, and are the **Earth**.

# THE EMPEROR’S EPISTLE

The **Purifying** <note>original: *Reinigende*.</note> powers are the **Diverting** <note>original: *Abdwenede*; likely referring to the power that turns or redirects substances during digestion or separation.</note> powers; namely, those that effect change are **Feminine**, and are warm and moist, and are of the **Air**.

The **Expelling** <note>original: *Austreibende*.</note> powers, however, are **Feminine**, and are cold and moist, and are the **Water**.

But the fifth is the **Life** <note>Refers to the *Quinta Essentia* or Quintessence, the "fifth essence" that transcends the four elements.</note>, which is of its own kind: neither warm nor moist, nor cold nor dry, neither **Man** nor **Woman**.

Therefore, so that you do not go astray in the **Work** <term>Work</term> <note>The *Magnum Opus*, the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, should you perhaps seek these powers in flowing waters or brooks, I will show you their true place. And therefore, when you wish to dissolve the **Power** <note>original: *Krafft*.</note> of cold water, you must coagulate it more firmly. And when you wish to make a water cold, you must make it more firmly warm. Unless you rectify the **Nature** of each individual power according to how you work, you will ruin the Work.

Accordingly, since the **Desiring** powers are dry and coloring, that is **Mascu-**

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the specific qualities and gendered associations of the alchemical "powers" or forces. # Epistle of the Emperor

The **Purifying** [powers] are the **Diverting** <note>original: *Abwendende*; forces that turn away or redirect impurities.</note> ones; namely, those that change are **Feminine**, and they are warm and moist, and they belong to the **Air**.

But the **Expelling** [powers] are **Feminine**, and they are cold and moist, and they are the **Water**.

But the fifth is **Life**, which is its own property; it is neither warm nor moist, nor cold nor dry, nor **Man** nor **Woman** <note>This "fifth" likely refers to the Quintessence (literally "fifth essence"), the pure, balanced core of matter that transcends the four elements and the duality of gender.</note>.

Therefore, so that you do not go astray in the **Work** <note>original: *Werck*; the "Great Work" of alchemy, the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, should you perhaps seek it in flowing waters or streams, I will show you its permanent place. And therefore, when you wish to dissolve the **Power** <note>original: *Krafft*.</note> of cold water, you must <term>coagulate</term> <note>original: *coagulirest*; to thicken or solidify a liquid into a curd-like or solid state.</note> it more firmly. And when you wish to make a water cold, you make it more firmly warm. Unless it be the case that you improve the **Nature** of each power according to how you work, you will ruin the **Work**.

Accordingly, as the desiring powers are dry and they color—that is **Masculine**—

<summary>This page defines the elemental and gendered qualities of the Purifying and Expelling powers, while identifying a "fifth" power—Life—that remains neutral and balanced. It offers practical (if paradoxical) alchemical advice on manipulating temperature and state, such as making a substance cold by applying heat.</summary>

### OF ALEXANDER

->**ALEXANDER'S EPISTLE**<-

**Wolf** is found in the **Rising**, and the **Dog** in the **Setting**; the latter has bitten the former, and the former has bitten the latter, and both become enraged and kill one another, until out of them is made a **Poison** and a **Theriac** <note>original: "Tyriack"—a famous ancient medicinal compound used as an antidote to poison. Our **White Camel** is the seventh in number among the great **philosophers**: the **Sun** with the **Moon**, **Jupiter**, **Mars**, and **Venus**—our **Venus** in our **Mercury**—and **Saturn** the seventh among them, in whom they are all joined together <note>The "White Camel" is a rare alchemical symbol for the purified matter that "carries" the work. The list of planets corresponds to the seven metals: Gold, Silver, Tin, Iron, Copper, Quicksilver, and Lead.</note>. This [Saturn] is a **Sword** and **Knife**, and a **cutting-tool** for the building, and the present enemy, and a vessel with the assistance of **Wine**. Our **Bishop** or **Presider** is like the color of veins or of **violets**, and the **soul** is yellow like the **Narcissus** or <term>Hypocistis: A parasitic plant (Cytinus hypocistis) used in ancient medicine; here it refers to a specific yellow-gold hue produced during the transformation.</term>, and then the **Stone** [turns] into a fast-flowing cast at the death of all creatures—I do not lie. Our example or model with the **whiteness of nature** is the **fire** and the **light** in the essence and being of **clarity**; the **fire** and the **light**
